Split-and-Conquer: Distributed Factor Modeling for High-Dimensional Matrix-Variate Time Series
Hangjin Jiang, Yuzhou Li, Zhaoxing Gao

Abstract
In this paper, we propose a distributed framework for reducing the dimensionality of high-dimensional, large-scale, heterogeneous matrix-variate time series data using a factor model. The data are first partitioned column-wise (or row-wise) and allocated to node servers, where each node estimates the row (or column) loading matrix via two-dimensional tensor PCA. These local estimates are then transmitted to a central server and aggregated, followed by a final PCA step to obtain the global row (or column) loading matrix estimator. Given the estimated loading matrices, the corresponding factor matrices are subsequently computed. Unlike existing distributed approaches, our framework preserves the latent matrix structure, thereby improving computational efficiency and enhancing information utilization. We also discuss row- and column-wise clustering procedures for settings in which the group memberships are unknown. Furthermore, we extend the analysis to unit-root nonstationary matrix-variate time series. Asymptotic properties of the proposed method are derived for the diverging dimension of the data in each computing unit and the sample size T T . Simulation results assess the computational efficiency and estimation accuracy of the proposed framework, and real data applications further validate its predictive performance.



Keywords
: Split-and-Conquer,
Matrix Factor Model, Large-scale Time Series, Principal Component Analysis, Heterogeneity

1
Introduction

Time series models with factor structures are widely applied across diverse fields, including finance, macroeconomics, and machine learning, due to their ability to represent large-scale time series through a small number of latent factors. Early research considered models with a fixed number of factors. For instance,
36
modeled asset returns as linear functions of macroeconomic risks, and
23
represented multinational current accounts using global and country-specific factors. With the growing focus on dimensionality reduction for large-scale data, subsequent research developed approaches to infer a low-dimensional factor structure.
6
introduced the approximate factor model employing principal component analysis (PCA) to estimate the latent factors, with important extensions by
38
,
37
, and
2
. To address strong temporal dependence, later research captured co-movements across multiple moments, leading to the dynamic factor model
(
14
;
15
;
16
)
and factor models with dynamically dependent factors
(
29
;
18
;
19
)
. When the number of factors is unknown, various methods have been proposed for its estimation, including the matrix rank test
(
30
;
11
)
, modified BIC
(
38
;
2
)
, multivariate AIC
(
14
)
, and eigenvalue ratio-based methods
(
29
;
1
)
.

While vector factor models are widely used, they are not well-suited for matrix time series because they ignore multi-dimensional structural information. Matrix time series have become increasingly important in fields such as neuroscience (e.g., large-scale fMRI data), economics (e.g., high-frequency trading records), and machine learning (e.g., parameter snapshots of pre-trained models).
39
introduced a matrix factor model that preserves the latent matrix structure, employing row and column loading matrices to capture the variance across the corresponding dimensions. Building on this model and two-dimensional tensor PCA,
8
proposed an
\alpha
-PCA estimation procedure to enhance estimation accuracy and applicability, while
40
introduced a projection method that further improves estimation accuracy.
24
developed a robust iterative estimation procedure, and
25
robustified the factor number estimation via eigenvalue ratios of generalized row/column matrix Kendall’s tau matrices. Additionally,
20
proposed a two-way transformed model providing solid statistical guarantees and improved interpretability.

Despite the effectiveness of factor models in dimensionality reduction for high-dimensional time series, storing and processing all data on a single machine are often impractical. Estimation procedures commonly require matrix operations involving billions or trillions of elements, making single-machine estimation infeasible. In practice, large-scale data are always distributed across multiple machines due to concerns over communication costs, privacy, and security. For example, high-frequency trading data are stored across exchanges, remote sensing data are massive and dispersed, and large-scale fMRI datasets are distributed across institutions to protect medical privacy. To address these challenges, distributed estimation procedures have been developed in which local estimators are computed on node servers, transmitted to a central server, and aggregated into global estimates. Early work by
35
introduced a distributed PCA method under a horizontal partitioning regime, where each node stores a subset of observations across all variables.
12
refined the distributed PCA and established solid statistical guarantees, while
22
and
9
proposed multi-round methods to reduce communication cost and improve computational efficiency. Additionally,
28
introduced a collective PCA method with a vertical partitioning regime, where each node stores all observations for a subset of variables, with further developments by
32
and
4
.

However, as previously discussed, distributed methods developed for vector-variate factor models perform sub-optimally when applied to matrix time series. This motivates the study on distributed estimation for matrix-variate factor models. A naive approach is to partition the matrix data along columns or rows, estimate the loading matrices within each block, and then aggregate the local results to obtain global estimates. Although this framework preserves the latent matrix structure, it has two notable limitations when both the row and column loading matrices are estimated from a single block. Without loss of generality, consider an equal column-wise partition. First, the estimation accuracy of the column loading matrix decreases substantially faster than that of the row loading matrix. Specifically, the former decays at a rate proportional to the square of the number of splits, whereas the latter declines only linearly (see Section
3
). Second, because each computing node uses all data rows, localized heterogeneity may be obscured. These limitations motivate the development of improved distributed estimation methods.

This paper proposes a distributed estimation procedure for high-dimensional matrix-variate factor models, introducing a new partitioning regime. We first partition the observations column-wise according to their grouping structure and assign each subset to a node server, where the row loading matrix is estimated using
\alpha
-PCA
(
8
)
. These local estimates are transmitted to a central server, where they are aggregated and followed by a final PCA step to obtain a global estimator. In parallel, the observations are partitioned row-wise to estimate the column loading matrix. The factor matrices are then calculated based on the estimated loading matrices. This framework extends existing estimation methods for the matrix-variate factor model to efficiently handle high-dimensional heterogeneous data. Unlike existing distributed approaches, it preserves the latent matrix structure, thereby improving computational efficiency and information utilization. When data groupings are unknown, we recommend several clustering strategies for distributed data allocation, while the eigenvalue ratio-based method
(
29
;
1
)
is used to estimate the number of factors. We establish rigorous statistical guarantees under mild conditions, demonstrating the consistency of the proposed estimators as the sample size and data dimension at each computing unit diverge, and we further derive asymptotic normality of the loading matrix estimators. Extensive empirical validations, including simulations and applications to the Fama–French Stock Return data and the OECD CPI data, confirm the effectiveness of our method. Across these studies, our approach achieves substantially higher computational efficiency and better predictive performance, while maintaining accuracy comparable to that of
\alpha
-PCA.

From a statistical perspective, our method extends
\alpha
-PCA
(
8
)
to accommodate high-dimensional heterogeneous settings, and we further establish asymptotic normality for both their estimators and our proposed estimators. In related work,
7
proposed a distributed tensor PCA method for large-scale tensor datasets, motivated by considerations similar to ours in employing distributed estimation. However, their double-loop singular value decomposition (SVD) framework is less efficient and less streamlined. Our method is also connected to the distributed hierarchical factor model
(
21
)
, particularly when both row and column loading matrices are estimated from column-wise (or row-wise) partitions. While the hierarchical model accommodates dispersed data and captures the commonality and heterogeneity across partitions, it converges more slowly and fails to preserve the latent matrix structure. Moreover,
27
, also based on the matrix factor model, exploited banded and general sparse structures in high-dimensional data to achieve dimensionality reduction, thereby enhancing interpretability and scalability. In contrast, our method imposes no specific structural assumptions and is applicable in more general settings.

The contributions of this paper are threefold. First, we propose a distributed estimation procedure for reducing the dimensionality of high-dimensional, large-scale, heterogeneous matrix-variate time series data using the matrix factor model. By preserving the matrix structure and employing the proposed partitioning regime, our method efficiently and accurately extracts low-dimensional latent factors while maintaining the overall common structure and respecting inter-group heterogeneity. Simulation results confirm that our approach substantially improves computational efficiency while maintaining high estimation accuracy, and real data applications further validate its predictive performance. Second, we establish rigorous statistical guarantees for the proposed method by deriving its asymptotic properties for the diverging sample size and data dimension at each computing unit. In particular, we obtain new results on the asymptotic normality of the
\alpha
-PCA loading estimators and derive the asymptotic normality of our loading estimators. Third, we extend the proposed method to accommodate unit-root nonstationary time series, enabling the identification of common stochastic trends, and provide corresponding statistical guarantees.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. Section
2
introduces our estimation methodology, and Section
3
presents its theoretical properties. Our framework is extended to unit-root nonstationary data in Section
4
. Numerical studies based on simulated and real datasets are reported in Section
5
. Section
6
provides some concluding remarks. All technical proofs and additional numerical results are presented in an online supplementary Appendix. Throughout the article, we use the following notation. For a
p\times 1
vector
{\mathbf{v}}=(v_{1},...,v_{p})^{\top},

||{\mathbf{v}}||_{2}=\|{\mathbf{v}}^{\top}\|_{2}=(\sum_{i=1}^{p}v_{i}^{2})^{1/2}
denotes the Euclidean norm and
\|{\mathbf{v}}\|_{\infty}=\max_{1\leq i\leq p}|v_{i}|
denotes the
\ell_{\infty}
-norm.
{\mathbf{I}}_{p}
denotes a
p\times p
identity matrix. For a matrix
{\mathbf{H}}=(h_{ij})\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
,
\|{\mathbf{H}}\|_{1}=\max_{j}\sum_{i}|h_{ij}|
,
\|{\mathbf{H}}\|_{\infty}=\max_{i,j}|h_{ij}|
,
\|{\mathbf{H}}\|_{\mathsf{F}}=\sqrt{\sum_{i,j}h_{ij}^{2}}
denotes the Frobenius norm, and
\|{\mathbf{H}}\|_{2}=\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}({\mathbf{H}}^{\top}{\mathbf{H}})}
denotes the operator norm, where
\lambda_{\max}(\cdot)
denotes the largest eigenvalue of a matrix.
{\mathbf{H}}^{l}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times n}
denotes the
l
-th row vector of a matrix
{\mathbf{H}}
. For a matrix time series
\{{\mathbf{H}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n};\ t\in[T]\}
,
\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
denotes its sample mean and
[T]
denotes the set
\{1,2,...,T\}
. We also use the notation
a\asymp b
to denote
a=O(b)
and
b=O(a)
.

2
Model and Methodology

2.1
Model Setting

Let
{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}
be an observable
p\times q
-dimensional stationary time series for
t=1,2,...,T
, and assume it admits a latent matrix factor structure:

\mathbf{Y}_{t}=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{F}_{t}\mathbf{C}^{\top}+\mathbf{E}_{t},\quad t=1,2,...,T,

(2.1)

where
\mathbf{F}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times r}
is the common factor matrix with dimensions
k\ll p
and
r\ll q
,
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times k}
and
\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times r}
are the row and column loading matrices respectively, capturing the common covariance, and
\mathbf{E}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
denotes the idiosyncratic component, assumed uncorrelated with
\mathbf{F}_{t}
.

It is well known that the triplet
[\mathbf{R},\mathbf{F}_{t},\mathbf{C}]
is not separately identifiable. Specifically, for any nonsingular matrices
\mathbf{H}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
and
\mathbf{H}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
, the triplet
[\mathbf{R},\mathbf{F}_{t},\mathbf{C}]
is equivalent to
[\mathbf{R}\mathbf{H}_{1},\mathbf{H}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{t}(\mathbf{H}_{2}^{-1})^{\top},\mathbf{C}\mathbf{H}_{2}]
under model (
2.1
). Following the restriction used in
8
, we assume that each row of the loading matrices is bounded, i.e.,

\left\|\mathbf{R}^{l_{1}}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)\quad\text{and}\quad\left\|\mathbf{C}^{l_{2}}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}(1),\quad l_{1}=1,2,...,p,\quad l_{2}=1,2,...,q.

Our estimation procedure yields the triplet
[\widehat{\mathbf{R}},\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t},\widehat{\mathbf{C}}]
, which serve as an estimator of
[\mathbf{R},\mathbf{F}_{t},\mathbf{C}]
. Specifically, there exist nonsingular matrices
\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{R}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
and
\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
, such that
\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}
estimates
\mathbf{R}\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{R}}
,
\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}
estimates
\mathbf{C}\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{C}}
, and
\widehat{{\mathbf{F}}}_{t}
estimates
\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{R}}^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{t}(\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{C}}^{-1})^{\top}
.

2.2
Estimation Procedure for Low-dimensional Case

Several estimation methods have been developed for model (
2.1
). Among them, the
\alpha
-PCA method in
8
is computationally efficient and easy to implement, as it applies two-dimensional tensor PCA to statistics based on the sample mean and covariance. We first outline this estimation procedure and then introduce our distributed approach developed for high-dimensional observations. Throughout this section, we assume that
k
and
r
are known, with their estimation discussed later. Define the
p\times p
and
q\times q
aggregation matrices,

\widehat{{\mathbf{M}}}_{{\mathbf{R}}}^{\alpha}=\frac{1}{pq}\left[(1+\alpha)\overline{\mathbf{Y}}\overline{\mathbf{Y}}^{\top}+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\mathbf{Y}_{t}-\overline{\mathbf{Y}})(\mathbf{Y}_{t}-\overline{\mathbf{Y}})^{\top}\right]

(2.2)

and

\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\alpha}=\frac{1}{pq}\left[(1+\alpha)\overline{\mathbf{Y}}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{Y}}+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\mathbf{Y}_{t}-\overline{\mathbf{Y}})^{\top}(\mathbf{Y}_{t}-\overline{\mathbf{Y}})\right],

(2.3)

where
\alpha

\in[-1,+\infty)
is a hyper-parameter controlling the weight of the two terms. The row and column loading matrix estimators,
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{\alpha}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{\alpha}
, are obtained as
\sqrt{p}
times the top
k
eigenvectors of
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{R}}^{\alpha}
and
\sqrt{q}
times the top
r
eigenvectors of
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\alpha}
, respectively. Let
\widetilde{\alpha}=\sqrt{\alpha+1}-1
, and define
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}=\mathbf{Y}_{t}+\widetilde{\alpha}\overline{\mathbf{Y}}
,
\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}_{t}=\mathbf{F}_{t}+\widetilde{\alpha}\overline{\mathbf{F}}
, and
\widetilde{\mathbf{E}}_{t}=\mathbf{E}_{t}+\widetilde{\alpha}\overline{\mathbf{E}}
, so that
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}={\mathbf{R}}\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}_{t}{\mathbf{C}}^{\top}+\widetilde{\mathbf{E}}_{t}
. Under this definition, Equations (
2.2
) and (
2.3
) are simplified to

\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{R}}^{\alpha}=\frac{1}{pqT}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}^{\top}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\alpha}=\frac{1}{pqT}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}.

2.3
Estimation Procedure for High-dimensional Case

In this section, we introduce our distributed estimation procedure, starting with known
k
and
r
. The first step is to estimate the row loading matrix
{\mathbf{R}}
. We assume that
{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}
admits a column-wise grouping structure,

{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{Y}_{1t}&\mathbf{Y}_{2t}&\cdots&\mathbf{Y}_{s_{1}t}\end{pmatrix},

where
\mathbf{Y}_{it}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times m_{i}}
for
i\in[s_{1}]
and
\sum_{i=1}^{s_{1}}m_{i}=q
. These blocks are assigned to
s_{1}
computing nodes. Under model (
2.1
), the block
{\mathbf{Y}}_{it}
follows the sub-model,

\mathbf{Y}_{it}=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{F}_{t}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\top}+\mathbf{E}_{it},\quad t=1,2,...,T,

(2.4)

where

\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{C}_{1}^{\top}&\mathbf{C}_{2}^{\top}&\cdots&\mathbf{C}_{s_{1}}^{\top}\end{pmatrix}=\mathbf{C}^{\top}\quad\text{and}\quad\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{E}_{1t}&\mathbf{E}_{2t}&\cdots&\mathbf{E}_{s_{1}t}\end{pmatrix}=\mathbf{E}_{t},

with
\mathbf{C}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{i}\times r}
and
\mathbf{E}_{it}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times m_{i}}
. At each node, we apply
\alpha
-PCA to its assigned data block to obtain a local estimator of
{\mathbf{R}}
. Specifically, for each
i\in[s_{1}]
, the estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{i}
is constructed by multiplying
\sqrt{p}
with the eigenvectors of
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{R}_{i}}
corresponding to its largest
k
eigenvalues in decreasing order, where

\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{R}_{i}}=\frac{1}{pm_{i}}\left[(1+\alpha)\overline{\mathbf{Y}}_{i}\overline{{\mathbf{Y}}}_{i}^{\top}+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\mathbf{Y}_{it}-\overline{\mathbf{Y}}_{i}\right)\left(\mathbf{Y}_{it}-\overline{\mathbf{Y}}_{i}\right)^{\top}\right]=\frac{1}{pm_{i}T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\widetilde{{\mathbf{Y}}}_{it}\widetilde{{\mathbf{Y}}}_{it}^{\top},

(2.5)

with
\overline{\mathbf{Y}}_{i}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{Y}_{it}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{it}=\mathbf{Y}_{it}+\widetilde{\alpha}\overline{\mathbf{Y}}_{i}
. The local estimators
\{\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{s_{1}}
are transmitted to a center server and aggregated by

\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{R}}=\frac{1}{ps_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{s_{1}}\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}_{i}\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}_{i}^{\top}.

(2.6)

The global estimator
\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}
is then obtained by multiplying
\sqrt{p}
with the eigenvectors of
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{R}}
corresponding to its largest k eigenvalues in decreasing order.

Next, we estimate the column loading matrix
{\mathbf{C}}
similarly. We assume that
{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}
also admits a row-wise grouping structure,

\mathbf{Y}_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{Z}_{1t}^{\top}&\mathbf{Z}_{2t}^{\top}&\cdots&\mathbf{Z}_{s_{2}t}^{\top}\end{pmatrix}^{\top},

where
\mathbf{Z}_{jt}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{j}\times q}
for
j\in[s_{2}]
and
\sum_{j=1}^{s_{2}}n_{j}=p
. These blocks are assigned to
s_{2}
computing nodes. By the transpose of model (
2.1
), the block
\mathbf{Z}_{jt}
follows another sub-model,

\mathbf{Z}_{jt}=\mathbf{R}_{j}\mathbf{F}_{t}\mathbf{C}^{\top}+\bm{\varepsilon}_{jt},\quad t=1,2,...,T,

(2.7)

where

\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{R}_{1}^{\top}&\mathbf{R}_{2}^{\top}&\cdots&\mathbf{R}_{s_{2}}^{\top}\end{pmatrix}=\mathbf{R}^{\top}\quad\text{and}\quad\begin{pmatrix}\bm{\varepsilon}_{1t}^{\top}&\bm{\varepsilon}_{2t}^{\top}&\cdots&\bm{\varepsilon}_{s_{2}t}^{\top}\end{pmatrix}=\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\top},

with
\mathbf{R}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{j}\times k}
and
\bm{\varepsilon}_{jt}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{j}\times q}
. Each node computes a local estimator of
{\mathbf{C}}
using
\alpha
-PCA. Specifically, for each
j\in[s_{2}]
, the estimator
\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j}
is formed by multiplying
\sqrt{q}
with the largest
r
eigenvectors of
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{C}_{j}}
, where

\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{C}_{j}}=\frac{1}{qn_{j}}\left[(1+\alpha)\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{j}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{j}+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\mathbf{Z}_{jt}-\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{j})^{\top}(\mathbf{Z}_{jt}-\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{j})\right]=\frac{1}{qn_{j}T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\widetilde{{\mathbf{Z}}}_{jt}^{\top}\widetilde{{\mathbf{Z}}}_{jt},

(2.8)

with
\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{j}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{jt}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{jt}=\mathbf{Z}_{jt}+\widetilde{\alpha}\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{j}
. The local estimators
\{\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{s_{2}}
are sent to a center server and aggregated by

\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{C}}=\frac{1}{qs_{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{s_{2}}\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j}\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j}^{\top}.

(2.9)

The global estimator
\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}
is then obtained by multiplying
\sqrt{q}
with the largest
r
eigenvectors of
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{C}}
. We summarize our estimation procedure in
Algorithm
1
, denoted as dPCA.

Input:

Observations
\{{\mathbf{Y}}_{t},t=1,2,...,T\}

Output:

Estimated loading matrices
\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}
and
\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}

Partition
{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}
column-wise into
\{{\mathbf{Y}}_{it}\}_{i=1}^{s_{1}}
, for
t=1,2,...,T

Partition
{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}
row-wise into
\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{jt}\}_{j=1}^{s_{2}}
, for
t=1,2,...,T

1

Do in parallel

2

Obtain
\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}_{i}
from
\{{\mathbf{Y}}_{it},t=1,2,...,T\}
employing
\alpha
-PCA, for
i=1,2,...,s_{1}

Obtain
\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j}
from
\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{jt},t=1,2,...,T\}
employing
\alpha
-PCA, for
j=1,2,...,s_{2}

3

Obtain
\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}
by multiplying
\sqrt{p}
with the top
k
eigenvectors of
\frac{1}{ps_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{s_{1}}\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}_{i}\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}_{i}^{\top}

Obtain
\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}
by multiplying
\sqrt{q}
with the top
r
eigenvectors of
\frac{1}{qs_{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{s_{2}}\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j}\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j}^{\top}

Algorithm 1

Distributed estimation for
{\mathbf{R}}
and
{\mathbf{C}}
(dPCA)

The proposed method substantially improves the computational efficiency of modeling high-dimensional time series data under model (
2.1
). Calculating
\widehat{{\mathbf{M}}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}
and
\widehat{{\mathbf{M}}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}
requires
\mathcal{O}(p^{2}Tm_{i})
and
\mathcal{O}(q^{2}Tn_{j})
operations, respectively, for
i\in[s_{1}]
and
j\in[s_{2}]
. The associated eigenvector matrices
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{i}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{j}
are obtained using the Lanczos algorithm with computational costs
\mathcal{O}(p^{2}k)
and
\mathcal{O}(q^{2}r)
. Similarly, Computing
\widehat{{\mathbf{M}}}_{{\mathbf{R}}}
and
\widehat{{\mathbf{M}}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}
requires
\mathcal{O}(p^{2}ks_{1})
and
\mathcal{O}(q^{2}rs_{2})
operations, followed by
\mathcal{O}(p^{2}k)
and
\mathcal{O}(q^{2}r)
to obtain
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}
. With parallel computation and sufficiently large
T
, the total complexity for estimating the loading matrices is
\mathcal{O}(p^{2}T\max_{i}m_{i}+q^{2}T\max_{j}n_{j})
. In contrast,
\alpha
-PCA requires
\mathcal{O}(p^{2}Tq+q^{2}Tp)
operations. Consequently, our method achieves considerable computational savings over
\alpha
-PCA.

2.4
Estimation of other unknown parameters

Using the loading matrix estimators
\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}
and
\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}
obtained from
Algorithm 1
, the common factor matrix
{\mathbf{F}}_{t}
is estimated by

\widehat{{\mathbf{F}}}_{t}=\frac{1}{pq}\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}^{\top}{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}.

(2.10)

The common component
{\mathbf{S}}_{t}={\mathbf{R}}{\mathbf{F}}_{t}{\mathbf{C}}^{\top}
is then estimated as

\widehat{{\mathbf{S}}}_{t}=\frac{1}{pq}\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}^{\top}{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}^{\top}.

(2.11)

We next estimate the factor numbers
k
and
r
using the eigenvalue ratio-based method of
29
and
1
. In the low-dimensional case,
k
is estimated by

\widehat{k}_{\alpha}=\text{arg}\max_{1\leq l\leq k_{\max}}\frac{\widehat{\lambda}^{\alpha}_{l}}{\widehat{\lambda}^{\alpha}_{l+1}},

(2.12)

where
\widehat{\lambda}^{\alpha}_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\widehat{\lambda}^{\alpha}_{p}\geq 0
are the ordered eigenvalues of
\widehat{{\mathbf{M}}}_{\mathbf{R}}^{\alpha}
, and
k_{\max}=\left\lfloor p/2\right\rfloor
following
29
. The estimator of
r
,
\widehat{r}_{\alpha}
, is obtained similarly from the eigenvalue ratios of
\widehat{{\mathbf{M}}}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\alpha}
. In the high-dimensional case, the row factor number estimators
\widehat{k}_{i}
(on node
i
) and
\widehat{k}
(on central server) are calculated using the eigenvalue ratios of
\widehat{{\mathbf{M}}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}
and
\widehat{{\mathbf{M}}}_{\mathbf{R}}
, respectively, for
i\in[s_{1}]
. Similarly, the column factor number estimators
\widehat{r}_{j}
(on node
j
) and
\widehat{r}
(on central server) are computed using the eigenvalue ratios of
\widehat{{\mathbf{M}}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}
and
\widehat{{\mathbf{M}}}_{\mathbf{C}}
, respectively, for
j\in[s_{2}]
.

2.5
Strategies for distributed data allocation

We examined in Section
2.3
how the numbers of columns
\{m_{i}\}_{i=1}^{s_{1}}
and rows
\{n_{j}\}_{j=1}^{s_{2}}
allocated to computing nodes affect computational efficiency, and we later evaluate their impact on estimation accuracy in Section
3
. When the data groupings are unknown, the assignment of rows or columns to each node, which also influences model performance, has not yet been discussed. We therefore discuss several strategies for distributed data allocation, which are further employed in the real data applications in Section
5.2
.

The first strategy allocates data according to external covariates. For example, when modeling multinational trade data, countries may be ordered by geographic distance or GDP rankings, and trade items by trade volume or value added, and then sequentially assigned to computing nodes. The second strategy allocates data by clustering. To mitigate the scaling effect in PCA, we recommend clustering data based on variance, following
21
. For the data rows
\{{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}^{l},l=1,2,...,p\}
, let
v_{l}
denote the norm of the auto-covariance matrix of the
l
-th row. By sorting
\{v_{l}\}_{l=1}^{p}
in decreasing order, the corresponding rows are reordered accordingly. The first
n_{1}
rows are allocated to the first node, the next
n_{2}
to the second node, and so on, until the last
n_{s_{2}}
rows are assigned to the
s_{2}
-th node. This approach is computationally efficient for high-dimensional time series. Alternative clustering approaches may instead use the Euclidean distance, Manhattan distance, or sample autocorrelations
(
5
)
.

3
Theoretical Properties

In this section, we establish the theoretical properties of the proposed method. We begin by introducing the assumptions required to derive these results. Most of them are adopted from
8
and
40
, or are standard in the factor model literature. Throughout,
C
denotes a generic positive constant whose value may vary across contexts.

Assumption 1
.

The vectorized process
\{\operatorname{vec}(\mathbf{F}_{t}),\operatorname{vec}(\mathbf{E}_{t})\}
is stationary and
\alpha
-mixing with the mixing coefficient
\alpha(h)
satisfying
\sum_{h=1}^{\infty}\alpha(h)^{1-2/\gamma}<\infty
for some
\gamma>2
, where

\alpha(h)=\sup_{\tau}\sup_{A\in\mathcal{F}_{-\infty}^{\tau},B\in\mathcal{F}_{\tau+h}^{\infty}}|P(A\cap B)-P(A)P(B)|,

with the
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{F}_{\tau}^{s}
generated by
\{(\operatorname{vec}(\mathbf{F}_{t}),\operatorname{vec}(\mathbf{E}_{t})):\tau\leq t\leq s\}
.

Assumption 2
.

For the common factor
{\mathbf{F}}_{t}
and the idiosyncratic component
{\mathbf{E}}_{t}
, we assume (a)
\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{F}_{t}\|_{2}^{4}\leq C
, for
t\in[T]
;

(b)
\mathbb{E}[e_{t,ij}]=0
and
\mathbb{E}|e_{t,ij}|^{8}\leq C
, for
i\in[p]
,
j\in[q]
, and
t\in[T]
;

(c)
{\mathbf{F}}_{t}
and
{\mathbf{E}}_{t}
are uncorrelated, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}[e_{t,ij}f_{s,lh}]=0
, for
l\in[k]
,
h\in[r]
,
i\in[p]
,
j\in[q]
, and
t,s\in[T]
.

Assumption 3
.

For the loading matrices
\mathbf{R}
and
\mathbf{C}
, we assume (a)
\|\mathbf{R}^{l_{1}}\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)
and
\|\mathbf{C}^{l_{2}}\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)
, for
l_{1}\in[p]
and
l_{2}\in[q]
;

(b) There exist positive definite matrices
\bm{\Omega}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
and
\bm{\Omega}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
, such that,
\|n_{i}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{R}_{i}-\bm{\Omega}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}\|_{2}\to 0
uniformly for
{\mathbf{R}}_{i}
as
n_{i},q\to\infty
, and
\|m_{j}^{-1}\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{C}_{j}-\bm{\Omega}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}\|_{2}\to 0
uniformly for
{\mathbf{C}}_{j}
as
m_{j},p\to\infty
, for
i\in[s_{2}]
and
j\in[s_{1}]
.

This assumption leads directly to the following result.

Proposition 1
.

Under Assumption
3
, there exist positive definite matrices
\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{R}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
and
\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
, such that,
\|p^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{\top}\mathbf{R}-\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{R}}\|_{2}\to 0
and
\|q^{-1}\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{C}-\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{C}}\|_{2}\to 0
, as
p,q\to\infty
.

Proof.

Define the positive definite matrix
\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{R}}=\sum_{i=1}^{s_{2}}\frac{n_{i}}{p}\bm{\Omega}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}
. We have

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{p}\mathbf{R}^{\top}\mathbf{R}-\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{R}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{1}{p}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{R}_{1}^{\top}&\cdots&\mathbf{R}_{s_{2}}^{\top}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{R}_{1}\\
\cdots\\
\mathbf{R}_{s_{2}}\end{pmatrix}-\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{R}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{s_{2}}\left(\mathbf{R}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{R}_{i}-n_{i}\bm{\Omega}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{s_{2}}\frac{n_{i}}{p}\left\|\frac{1}{n_{i}}\mathbf{R}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{R}_{i}-\bm{\Omega}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}\right\|_{2}\longrightarrow 0.

The last step is based on Assumption
3
(b). The proof for
{\mathbf{C}}
is similar, thus omitted.
∎

Assumption 4
.

For the idiosyncratic component
{\mathbf{E}}_{t}
, we assume (a)
\|\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{E}}\|_{1}\leq C
and
\|\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{E}}\|_{1}\leq C
, where
\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{E}}=\mathbb{E}[\frac{1}{qT}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{E}_{t}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\top}]
and
\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{E}}=\mathbb{E}[\frac{1}{pT}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{E}_{t}]
;

(b)
\sum_{l=1}^{p}\sum_{h=1}^{q}|\mathbb{E}[e_{t,lj}e_{t,ih}]|\leq C
, for
i\in[p]
,
j\in[q]
, and
t\in[T]
;

(c)
\sum_{m=1}^{p}\sum_{s=1}^{T}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h=1\\
h\neq j\end{subarray}}^{q}|\operatorname{cov}[e_{t,ij}e_{t,lj},e_{s,ih}e_{s,mh}]|\leq C
, for
i,l\in[p]
,
j\in[q]
, and
t\in[T]
;

(d)
\sum_{m=1}^{q}\sum_{s=1}^{T}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\neq i\end{subarray}}^{p}|\operatorname{cov}[e_{t,ij}e_{t,ih},e_{s,lj}e_{s,lm}]|\leq C
, for
i\in[p]
,
j,h\in[q]
, and
t\in[T]
.

Assumption 5
.

There exist
m>2
,
1<a,b<\infty
with
1/a+1/b=1
, such that:

(a)
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{F}_{t}^{l}\|_{2}^{ma}]\leq C
,
\mathbb{E}[|\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\sum_{j=1}^{q}e_{t,ij}|^{mb}]=\mathcal{O}(1)
, and
\mathbb{E}[\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\mathbf{C}^{j}e_{t,ij}\|_{2}^{mb}]=\mathcal{O}(1)
, for
l\in[k]
,
i\in[p]
, and
t\in[T]
;

(b)
\mathbb{E}[\|(\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\top})^{h}\|_{2}^{ma}]\leq C
,
\mathbb{E}[|\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\sum_{i=1}^{p}e_{t,ij}|^{mb}]=\mathcal{O}(1)
, and
\mathbb{E}[\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathbf{R}^{i}e_{t,ij}\|_{2}^{mb}]=\mathcal{O}(1)
, for
h\in[r]
,
j\in[q]
, and
t\in[T]
;

(c)
\mathbb{E}[|\frac{1}{\sqrt{pq}}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sum_{j=1}^{q}e_{t,ij}|^{mb}]=\mathcal{O}(1)
and
\mathbb{E}[\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{pq}}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sum_{j=1}^{q}(\mathbf{R}^{i})^{\top}\mathbf{C}^{j}e_{t,ij}\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{mb}]=\mathcal{O}(1)
, for
t\in[T]
.

Assumption
1
imposes standard temporal dependence conditions on
{\mathbf{E}}_{t}
and
{\mathbf{F}}_{t}
(e.g.,
39
,
39
), ensuring their asymptotic independence and controlling the variance of partial sums. Assumption
2
(a)-(b) guarantee that
{\mathbf{F}}_{t}
and
{\mathbf{E}}_{t}
are well defined, respectively, as is typical in factor model settings (e.g.,
2
,
2
), while Assumption
2
(c) ensures their mutual uncorrelatedness. Assumption
3
, similar to Assumption B in
2
, guarantees the non-trivial contribution of each factor to the common variance. This assumption justifies our data partitioning regime and is sufficient for Assumption 3 in
8
. Assumption
4
(a)-(b) allows for weak dependence of
{\mathbf{E}}_{t}
across time, rows, and columns, extending the assumptions in
37
and
15
to matrix data, and aligning with Assumption D.2 in
40
. Assumption
4
(c)-(d), similar to Assumption M1(c) in
37
, controls the magnitude of the fourth moments of
{\mathbf{E}}_{t}
. Finally, Assumption
5
, adopted from
8
, further regulates the dependence of
{\mathbf{F}}_{t}
and
{\mathbf{E}}_{t}
across rows and columns.

To present our theoretical results, we introduce additional notations. Let
\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
and
\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
denote diagonal matrices consisting of the
k
and
r
largest eigenvalues of
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{R}_{i}}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{C}_{j}}
, respectively, in decreasing order, for
i\in[s_{1}]
and
j\in[s_{2}]
. Define the invertible auxiliary matrices
{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
and
{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
by

{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}=\frac{1}{pm_{i}T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\widetilde{{\mathbf{F}}}_{t}{\mathbf{C}}_{i}^{\top}{\mathbf{C}}_{i}\widetilde{{\mathbf{F}}}_{t}^{\top}{\mathbf{R}}^{\top}\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}_{i}\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}^{-1}\ \ \text{and}\ \ {\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}=\frac{1}{qn_{j}T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\widetilde{{\mathbf{F}}}_{t}^{\top}{\mathbf{R}}_{j}^{\top}{\mathbf{R}}_{j}\widetilde{{\mathbf{F}}}_{t}{\mathbf{C}}^{\top}\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j}\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}^{-1}.

By Theorem 3.3 of
40
, under Assumptions
1
-
5
with known
k
and
r
, as
m_{i},n_{j},T\to\infty
, it follows that

\frac{1}{p}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{i}-\mathbf{RH}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}\right\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{2}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(\frac{1}{m_{i}T}+\frac{1}{p^{2}}\right)\ \ \text{and}\ \ \frac{1}{q}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{j}-\mathbf{CH}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}\right\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{2}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(\frac{1}{n_{j}T}+\frac{1}{q^{2}}\right).

Next, let
\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{{\mathbf{R}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
and
\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
denote diagonal matrices consisting of the
k
and
r
largest eigenvalues of
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{R}}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{C}}
, respectively, in decreasing order. Define the invertible auxiliary matrices
{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{R}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
and
{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
by

{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{R}}}=\frac{1}{ps_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{s_{1}}{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}^{\top}{\mathbf{R}}^{\top}\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{{\mathbf{R}}}^{-1}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}=\frac{1}{qs_{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{s_{2}}{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}^{\top}{\mathbf{C}}^{\top}\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{-1}.

Following
8
, an additional assumption is required to control the magnitudes of
{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}
and
{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}
, which further ensures the boundedness for
{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{R}}}
and
{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}
.

Assumption 6
.

We assume (a) The eigenvalues of
\bm{\Omega}_{{\mathbf{R}}}\widetilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{FC}_{i}}
and
\bm{\Omega}_{{\mathbf{C}}}\widetilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{FR}_{j}}
are distinct, for
i\in[s_{1}]
,
j\in[s_{2}]
; (b) The eigenvalues of
\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{R}}\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathbf{R}}}
and
\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{C}}\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathbf{C}}}
are distinct.

The matrices
\widetilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{FC}_{i}}=\frac{1}{m_{i}}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{C}_{i}\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}_{t}^{\top}]
and
\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathbf{R}}}=\frac{1}{s_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{s_{1}}\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{R}_{i}}^{-1}(\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{R}_{i}}^{-1})^{\top}
, where
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{R}_{i}}=\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{R}_{i}}^{1/2}\Psi_{\mathbf{R}_{i}}^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{FC}_{i}}^{-1/2}
. Here
\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{R}_{i}}
is the diagonal matrix of eigenvalues (ordered decreasingly) of
\widetilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{FC}_{i}}^{1/2}\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{R}}\widetilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{FC}_{i}}^{1/2}
, and
\Psi_{\mathbf{R}_{i}}
denotes the corresponding eigenvectors satisfying
\Psi_{\mathbf{R}_{i}}^{\top}\Psi_{\mathbf{R}_{i}}=\mathbf{I}
. The matrices
\widetilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{FR}_{j}}
and
\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathbf{C}}}
are similarly defined.

Assumption
6
(a) controls the magnitudes of the auxiliary matrices, and Assumption
6
also ensures the asymptotic stability of
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathbf{R}}
and
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathbf{C}}
. For convenience, define
\overline{m}=\max_{1\leq i\leq s_{1}}m_{i}
,
\overline{n}=\max_{1\leq j\leq s_{2}}n_{j}
,
\underline{m}=\min_{1\leq i\leq s_{1}}m_{i}
, and
\underline{n}=\min_{1\leq j\leq s_{2}}n_{j}
. Under these assumptions and notations, we establish the following results.

Theorem 1
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
6
(a) with known
k
and
r
, as
\underline{m},\underline{n},T\rightarrow\infty
, we have

\frac{1}{p}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{R}}-\mathbf{RH}_{\mathbf{R}}\right\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{2}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(\frac{1}{\underline{m}T}+\frac{1}{p^{2}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{q}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{C}}-\mathbf{CH}_{\mathbf{C}}\right\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{2}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(\frac{1}{\underline{n}T}+\frac{1}{q^{2}}\right).

Consequently,

\frac{1}{p}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{R}}-\mathbf{RH}_{\mathbf{R}}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(\frac{1}{\underline{m}T}+\frac{1}{p^{2}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{q}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{C}}-\mathbf{CH}_{\mathbf{C}}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(\frac{1}{\underline{n}T}+\frac{1}{q^{2}}\right).

Theorem 2
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
6
(a) with known
k
and
r
, as
\underline{m},\underline{n},T\rightarrow\infty
, we have

\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}-\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{R}}^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{t}(\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{C}}^{-1})^{\top}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\underline{m}T}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\underline{n}T}}+\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}\right).

Theorem 3
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
6
(a) with known
k
and
r
, as
\underline{m},\underline{n},T\rightarrow\infty
, we have

\widehat{{\mathbf{S}}}_{t,ij}-{\mathbf{S}}_{t,ij}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\underline{m}T}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\underline{n}T}}+\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}\right),\quad i=1,2,...,p,\quad j=1,2,...,q.

Remark 1
.

(i) Theorems
1
-
3
imply that the estimation accuracy of our method improves as
\underline{m}
and
\underline{n}
increase, while section
2.3
shows that smaller
\overline{m}
and
\overline{n}
reduce the computational cost. Together, these results reveal a trade-off between computational efficiency and estimation accuracy when allocating data across computing nodes;

(ii) Table
3.1
summarizes the convergence rates of loading matrix estimators from our procedure,
8
, and
40
. The rates established in Theorem
1
are comparable to those approaches. In particular, when
s_{1}=s_{2}=1
, our convergence rates match those of most methods, whereas the projection estimator converges faster due to its projection techniques. Notably, our method can also use the projection estimator in place of the
\alpha
-PCA estimator on each node, yielding faster convergence rates similar to theirs. The detailed derivations are omitted. These comparisons indicate that our distributed framework still preserves high estimation accuracy compared with single-machine approaches;

(iii) Our estimation procedure within each node can also be viewed as a projection framework. However, the estimators of
40
converge faster by employing initial estimators
\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}^{\text{I}}
and
\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}^{\text{I}}
as projection matrices. In both methods, the row loading matrix is estimated by multiplying the largest
k
eigenvalues of the column sample covariance matrix by
\sqrt{p}
. Our method constructs the covariance matrix on the first node as

\widehat{{\mathbf{M}}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{1}}=\frac{1}{pm_{1}T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\widetilde{{\mathbf{Y}}}_{1t}\widetilde{{\mathbf{Y}}}_{1t}^{\top}=\frac{1}{pm_{1}T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\widetilde{{\mathbf{Y}}}_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{e}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{e}_{m_{1}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{e}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{e}_{m_{1}}\end{pmatrix}^{\top}\widetilde{{\mathbf{Y}}}_{t}^{\top},

where
{\mathbf{e}}_{l}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times 1}
denotes the
l
-th standard basis vector for
l\in[m_{1}]
. In contrast,
40
compute the covariance matrix as

\widehat{{\mathbf{M}}}_{{\mathbf{R}}}^{\text{P}}=\frac{1}{Tpq^{2}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}{{\mathbf{Y}}}_{t}\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}^{\text{I}}(\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}^{\text{I}})^{\top}{{\mathbf{Y}}}_{t}^{\top},

where
\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}^{\text{I}}\overset{\mathcal{P}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\text{I}}
under the assumptions
\frac{1}{q}{\mathbf{C}}^{\top}{\mathbf{C}}\overset{\mathcal{P}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathbf{I}}
and
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\text{I}}({\mathbf{H}}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\text{I}})^{\top}\overset{\mathcal{P}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathbf{I}}
. The rapid convergence of the projection estimator relies on this consistency property of the projection matrix
\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}^{\text{I}}
, which does not hold for our projection matrix.

Table 3.1
:

This table reports the convergence rate of loading matrix estimators. “
\alpha
-PCA” refers to the estimators introduced by
8
, while “IE” and “PE” denote the initial and projection estimators proposed by
40
, respectively.

Next, we study asymptotic distributions of
\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}-{\mathbf{R}}{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathbf{R}}
and
\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}-{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathbf{C}}
. We begin by introducing several notations used in the subsequent analysis. Let
\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{R}}
and
\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{C}}
denote the diagonal matrices consisting of eigenvalues of
{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{HR}}^{1/2}\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{R}}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{HR}}^{1/2}
and
{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{HC}}^{1/2}\bm{\Omega}_{\mathbf{C}}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{HC}}^{1/2}
, respectively, in decreasing order. Let
\Psi_{\mathbf{R}}
and
\Psi_{\mathbf{C}}
denote the corresponding eigenvector matrices, satisfying
\Psi_{\mathbf{R}}^{\top}\Psi_{\mathbf{R}}=\mathbf{I}
and
\Psi_{\mathbf{C}}^{\top}\Psi_{\mathbf{C}}=\mathbf{I}
. Define
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{R}}=\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{R}}^{1/2}\Psi_{\mathbf{R}}^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{HR}}^{-1/2}
and
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{C}}=\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1/2}\Psi_{\mathbf{C}}^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{HC}}^{-1/2}
. For each
i,l\in[p]
and
j\in[s_{1}]
, define

\displaystyle{\mathbf{G}}_{i}^{j,l}=\begin{cases}\sqrt{\frac{\overline{m}}{m_{j}}}\Big[(\mathbf{R}^{i}{\mathbf{Q}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j}}^{-1})^{\top}{\mathbf{R}}^{i}+\sum_{\tau=1}^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{\tau}{\mathbf{Q}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j}}^{-1})^{\top}{\mathbf{R}}^{\tau}\Big]{\mathbf{Q}}_{{\mathbf{R}}}^{-1}{{\mathbf{V}}}_{\mathbf{R}}^{-1},&\quad l={i},\\
\sqrt{\frac{\overline{m}}{m_{j}}}(\mathbf{R}^{i}{\mathbf{Q}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j}}^{-1})^{\top}{\mathbf{R}}^{l}{\mathbf{Q}}_{{\mathbf{R}}}^{-1}{{\mathbf{V}}}_{\mathbf{R}}^{-1},&\quad l\neq{i}.\end{cases}

Analogously, for each
i^{\prime},l^{\prime}\in[q]
and
j^{\prime}\in[s_{2}]
, define

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime},l^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}\sqrt{\frac{\overline{n}}{n_{j^{\prime}}}}\Big[(\mathbf{C}^{i^{\prime}}{\mathbf{Q}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j^{\prime}}}^{-1})^{\top}{\mathbf{C}}^{i^{\prime}}+\sum_{\tau^{\prime}=1}^{q}(\mathbf{C}^{\tau^{\prime}}{\mathbf{Q}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j^{\prime}}}^{-1})^{\top}{\mathbf{C}}^{\tau^{\prime}}\Big]{\mathbf{Q}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{-1}{{\mathbf{V}}}_{\mathbf{C}}^{-1},&\quad l^{\prime}={i^{\prime}},\\
\sqrt{\frac{\overline{n}}{n_{j^{\prime}}}}(\mathbf{C}^{i^{\prime}}{\mathbf{Q}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}^{-1})^{\top}{\mathbf{C}}^{l^{\prime}}{\mathbf{Q}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{-1}{{\mathbf{V}}}_{\mathbf{C}}^{-1},&\quad l^{\prime}\neq{i^{\prime}}.\end{cases}

The following theorem establishes the asymptotic normality of
\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}-{\mathbf{R}}{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathbf{R}}
and
\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}-{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathbf{C}}
.

Theorem 4
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
6
with known
k
and
r
, as
\underline{m},\underline{n},T\to\infty
, we have

(a)

If
{\sqrt{\overline{m}T}}/{p}=\mathbf{o}(1)
and
{\overline{m}}/{\underline{m}}=\mathcal{O}(1)
, then for any
i\in[p]
,

\sqrt{\overline{m}T}\left(\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}^{i}-{\mathbf{R}}^{i}{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathbf{R}}\right)^{\top}\overset{\mathcal{D}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}\left(\mathbf{0},\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}\right),

where the asymptotic covariance matrix is

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p^{2}s_{1}^{2}}\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{s_{1}}\sum_{l_{1},l_{2}=1}^{p}({\mathbf{G}}_{i}^{j_{1},l_{1}})^{\top}{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1}}}^{-1}{\mathbf{Q}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1}}}\left(\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}^{1,1}+\alpha\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}^{1,2}\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}^{\top}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\alpha\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}^{2,1}+\alpha^{2}\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}^{2,2}\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}^{\top}\right){\mathbf{Q}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{2}}}^{\top}{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{2}}}^{-1}{\mathbf{G}}_{i}^{j_{2},l_{2}},

with

\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}^{1,1}=\underset{\underline{m},T\rightarrow\infty}{\operatorname{plim}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{m_{j_{1}}m_{j_{2}}}T}\sum_{t,s=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbf{F}_{t}\mathbf{C}_{j_{1}}^{\top}(\mathbf{E}_{j_{1}t}^{l_{1}})^{\top}\mathbf{E}_{j_{2}s}^{l_{2}}\mathbf{C}_{j_{2}}\mathbf{F}_{s}^{\top}\Big],

\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}^{1,2}=(\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}^{2,1})^{\top}=\underset{\underline{m},T\rightarrow\infty}{\operatorname{plim}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{m_{j_{1}}m_{j_{2}}}T}\sum_{t,s=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbf{F}_{t}\mathbf{C}_{j_{1}}^{\top}(\mathbf{E}_{j_{1}t}^{l_{1}})^{\top}\mathbf{E}_{j_{2}s}^{l_{2}}\mathbf{C}_{j_{2}}\Big],

and

\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}^{2,2}=\underset{\underline{m},T\rightarrow\infty}{\operatorname{plim}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{m_{j_{1}}m_{j_{2}}}T}\sum_{t,s=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbf{C}_{j_{1}}^{\top}(\mathbf{E}_{j_{1}t}^{l_{1}})^{\top}\mathbf{E}_{j_{2}s}^{l_{2}}\mathbf{C}_{j_{2}}\Big].

(b)

If
{\sqrt{\overline{n}T}}/{q}=\mathbf{o}(1)
and
{\overline{n}}/{\underline{n}}=\mathcal{O}(1)
, then for any
{i^{\prime}}\in[q]
,

\sqrt{\overline{n}T}\left(\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}^{i^{\prime}}-{\mathbf{C}}^{i^{\prime}}{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)^{\top}\overset{\mathcal{D}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}\left(\mathbf{0},\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{i^{\prime}}}\right),

where the asymptotic covariance matrix is

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{i^{\prime}}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q^{2}s_{2}^{2}}\sum_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime}=1}^{s_{2}}\sum_{l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}=1}^{q}({\mathbf{P}}_{i^{\prime}}^{j_{1}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime}})^{\top}{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime}}}^{-1}{\mathbf{Q}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime}}}\left(\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}}}^{1,1}+\alpha\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}}}^{1,2}\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\alpha\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}^{\top}\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}}}^{2,1}+\alpha^{2}\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}^{\top}\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}}}^{2,2}\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}\right){\mathbf{Q}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{2}^{\prime}}}^{\top}{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{2}^{\prime}}}^{-1}{\mathbf{P}}_{i^{\prime}}^{j_{2}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}},

with
\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}}}^{1,1}
,
\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}}}^{1,2}
,
\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}}}^{2,1}
, and
\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}}}^{2,2}
defined similarly.

Remark 2
.

Theorem
4
presents the asymptotic normality of our loading matrix estimators, consistent with the fact that the estimators of
8
and
40
are also asymptotically normal. Our proof first extends their results to derive the joint asymptotic distribution of all rows of
\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}_{j}-{{\mathbf{R}}}{\mathbf{H}}_{{{\mathbf{R}}}_{j}}
,
j\in[s_{1}]
. We then express
\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}-{{\mathbf{R}}}{\mathbf{H}}_{{{\mathbf{R}}}}
as a linear combination of
\{\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}_{j}-{{\mathbf{R}}}{\mathbf{H}}_{{{\mathbf{R}}}_{j}}\}_{j}
, yielding the asymptotic normality of its row vectors. The argument for
\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}
follows similarly.

Finally, we introduce consistent estimators for the asymptotic covariance matrices in Theorem
4
. For any
j_{1},j_{2}\in[s_{1}]
,
j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime}\in[s_{2}]
,
l_{1},l_{2}\in[p]
, and
l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}\in[q]
, define

\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}={\mathbf{Q}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1}}}(\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}^{1,1}+\alpha\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}^{1,2}\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}^{\top}+\alpha\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}^{2,1}+\alpha^{2}\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}^{2,2}\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}^{\top}){\mathbf{Q}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{2}}}^{\top},

and

\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}}}={\mathbf{Q}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime}}}(\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}}}^{1,1}+\alpha\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}}}^{1,2}\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}+\alpha\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}^{\top}\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}}}^{2,1}+\alpha^{2}\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}^{\top}\mathbf{\Phi}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}}}^{2,2}\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}){\mathbf{Q}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{2}^{\prime}}}^{\top}.

With these definitions, the asymptotic covariance matrices simplify to

\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}=\frac{1}{p^{2}s_{1}^{2}}\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{s_{1}}\sum_{l_{1},l_{2}=1}^{p}({\mathbf{G}}_{i}^{j_{1},l_{1}})^{\top}{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1}}}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{2}}}^{-1}{\mathbf{G}}_{i}^{j_{2},l_{2}},

and

\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{i^{\prime}}}=\frac{1}{q^{2}s_{2}^{2}}\sum_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime}=1}^{s_{2}}\sum_{l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}=1}^{q}({\mathbf{P}}_{i^{\prime}}^{j_{1}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime}})^{\top}{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime}}}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}}}{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime}}}^{-1}{\mathbf{P}}_{i^{\prime}}^{j_{1}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime}}.

We estimate
\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}
and
\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}}}
using the heteroskedasticity and autocorrelation consistent (HAC) estimators
(
33
)
. Specifically, for some
v\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
v/(\overline{m}T)^{1/4}=\mathbf{o}(1)
, the HAC estimator of
\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}
is defined as

\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}={\mathbf{D}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}^{0}+\sum_{\tau=1}^{v}\left(1-\frac{\tau}{1+v}\right)\left[{\mathbf{D}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}^{\tau}+({\mathbf{D}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}^{\tau})^{\top}\right],

where

\displaystyle{\mathbf{D}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}^{\tau}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m_{j_{1}}m_{j_{2}}}T}\sum_{t=1+\tau}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}{\mathbf{I}}&\alpha\overline{\widehat{{\mathbf{F}}}}_{j_{1},t}\end{pmatrix}\times

\displaystyle\ \ \ \begin{pmatrix}\widehat{{\mathbf{F}}}_{j_{1},t}(\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j_{1}}^{*})^{\top}(\widehat{{\mathbf{E}}}_{j_{1}t}^{l_{1}})^{\top}\widehat{{\mathbf{E}}}_{j_{2}(t-\tau)}^{l_{2}}\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j_{2}}^{*}\widehat{{\mathbf{F}}}_{j_{2},t-\tau}&\widehat{{\mathbf{F}}}_{j_{1},t}(\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j_{1}}^{*})^{\top}(\widehat{{\mathbf{E}}}_{j_{1}t}^{l_{1}})^{\top}\widehat{{\mathbf{E}}}_{j_{2}(t-\tau)}^{l_{2}}\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j_{2}}^{*}\\
(\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j_{1}}^{*})^{\top}(\widehat{{\mathbf{E}}}_{j_{1}t}^{l_{1}})^{\top}\widehat{{\mathbf{E}}}_{j_{2}(t-\tau)}^{l_{2}}\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j_{2}}^{*}\widehat{{\mathbf{F}}}_{j_{2},t-\tau}&(\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j_{1}}^{*})^{\top}(\widehat{{\mathbf{E}}}_{j_{1}t}^{l_{1}})^{\top}\widehat{{\mathbf{E}}}_{j_{2}(t-\tau)}^{l_{2}}\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j_{2}}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\mathbf{I}}\\
\alpha\overline{\widehat{{\mathbf{F}}}}_{j_{2},t}^{\top}\end{pmatrix}.

Here
\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j}^{*}
is an
\alpha
-PCA estimator of
{\mathbf{C}}_{j}
from the
j
-th sub-model (
2.4
),
\widehat{{\mathbf{F}}}_{j,t}=\frac{1}{pm_{j}}\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}_{j}^{\top}{\mathbf{Y}}_{jt}\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j}^{*}
, and
\widehat{{\mathbf{E}}}_{jt}={\mathbf{Y}}_{jt}-\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}_{j}\widehat{{\mathbf{F}}}_{j,t}(\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j}^{*})^{\top}
, for
j\in[s_{1}]
. The estimator
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}}}
is defined similarly. The next theorem establishes the consistency of
\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}
and
\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{i^{\prime}}}
.

Theorem 5
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
6
with known
k
and
r
, as
\underline{m},\underline{n},T\to\infty
,
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{i^{\prime}}}
are consistent estimators of
\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}
and
\bm{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{i^{\prime}}}
, respectively, for any
i\in[p]
and
i^{\prime}\in[q]
, where

\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}=\frac{1}{p^{2}s_{1}^{2}}\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{s_{1}}\sum_{l_{1},l_{2}=1}^{p}(\widehat{{\mathbf{G}}}_{i}^{j_{1},l_{1}})^{\top}\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1}}}^{-1}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{1},j_{2},l_{1},l_{2}}}\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j_{2}}}^{-1}\widehat{{\mathbf{G}}}_{i}^{j_{2},l_{2}},

and

\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{i^{\prime}}}=\frac{1}{q^{2}s_{2}^{2}}\sum_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime}=1}^{s_{2}}\sum_{l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}=1}^{q}(\widehat{{\mathbf{P}}}_{i^{\prime}}^{j_{1}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime}})^{\top}\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime}}}^{-1}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime}}}\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j_{1}^{\prime}}}^{-1}\widehat{{\mathbf{P}}}_{i^{\prime}}^{j_{1}^{\prime},l_{1}^{\prime}}.

The matrices
\widehat{{\mathbf{G}}}_{i}^{j,l}
and
\widehat{{\mathbf{P}}}_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime},l^{\prime}}
are given by

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathbf{G}}}_{i}^{j,l}=\begin{cases}\sqrt{\frac{\overline{m}}{m_{j}}}\Big[(\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{i}_{j})^{\top}\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}^{i}+\sum_{\tau=1}^{p}(\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{\tau}_{j})^{\top}\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}^{\tau}\Big]\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{\mathbf{R}}^{-1},&\quad l={i},\\
\sqrt{\frac{\overline{m}}{m_{j}}}(\widehat{\mathbf{R}}^{i}_{j})^{\top}\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}^{l}\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{\mathbf{R}}^{-1},&\quad l\neq{i},\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathbf{P}}}_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime},l^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}\sqrt{\frac{\overline{n}}{n_{j^{\prime}}}}\Big[(\widehat{\mathbf{C}}^{i^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}})^{\top}\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}^{i^{\prime}}+\sum_{\tau^{\prime}=1}^{q}(\widehat{\mathbf{C}}^{\tau^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}})^{\top}\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}^{\tau^{\prime}}\Big]\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{\mathbf{C}}^{-1},&\quad l^{\prime}={i^{\prime}},\\
\sqrt{\frac{\overline{n}}{n_{j^{\prime}}}}(\widehat{\mathbf{C}}^{i^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}})^{\top}\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}^{l^{\prime}}\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{\mathbf{C}}^{-1},&\quad l^{\prime}\neq{i^{\prime}}.\end{cases}

4
Extension to Unit-Root Nonstationary Time Series

In this section, we apply the proposed method to unit-root nonstationary time series. Let the observations
\{{\mathbf{Y}}_{t},t=1,2,...,T\}
be an
\text{I}(1)
process, that is,
{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}-{\mathbf{Y}}_{t-1}
is stationary. Following
34
, we assume that
{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}
admits the latent matrix factor structure,

{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}={\mathbf{R}}{\mathbf{F}}_{t}{\mathbf{C}}^{\top}+{\mathbf{E}}_{t},\quad t=1,2,...,T,

where the common component captures all stochastic trends. The common factor
{\mathbf{F}}_{t}
is assumed to be
\text{I}(1)
, and the idiosyncratic component
{\mathbf{E}}_{t}
is stationary with zero mean. This assumption is weaker than that imposed in
34
.

We next show that the proposed method remains valid under the above assumptions. The numbers of factors
k
and
r
are assumed to be known, as their estimators introduced in Section
2
are consistent by Theorem 3.3 of
31
. To establish the consistency of the estimated loading matrices, factor matrix, and common component, we introduce the following notations for the unit-root setting. For each
i\in[s_{1}]
and
j\in[s_{2}]
, let
\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
and
\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
denote diagonal matrices consisting of the
k
and
r
largest eigenvalues of
\frac{1}{T}\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{R}_{i}}
and
\frac{1}{T}\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{C}_{j}}
, respectively, in decreasing order. Define the auxiliary matrices

{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}^{*}=\frac{1}{pm_{i}T^{2}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\widetilde{{\mathbf{F}}}_{t}{\mathbf{C}}_{i}^{\top}{\mathbf{C}}_{i}\widetilde{{\mathbf{F}}}_{t}^{\top}{\mathbf{R}}^{\top}\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}_{i}(\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}^{*})^{-1},

and

{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}^{*}=\frac{1}{qn_{j}T^{2}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\widetilde{{\mathbf{F}}}_{t}^{\top}{\mathbf{R}}_{j}^{\top}{\mathbf{R}}_{j}\widetilde{{\mathbf{F}}}_{t}{\mathbf{C}}^{\top}\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j}(\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}^{*})^{-1}.

Lemma 5 in Appendix A shows that our estimators
\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}_{i}
and
\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j}
consistently estimate
{\mathbf{R}}{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}^{*}
and
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}^{*}
, respectively. Next, let
\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{{\mathbf{R}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
and
\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
denote diagonal matrices consisting of the
k
and
r
largest eigenvalues of
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{R}}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}_{\mathbf{C}}
, respectively, in decreasing order. Define the auxiliary matrices

{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{R}}}^{*}=\frac{1}{ps_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{s_{1}}{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}^{*}({\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}^{*})^{\top}{\mathbf{R}}^{\top}\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{{\mathbf{R}}}^{-1}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{*}=\frac{1}{qs_{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{s_{2}}{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}^{*}({\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}^{*})^{\top}{\mathbf{C}}^{\top}\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}\widehat{{\mathbf{V}}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{-1}.

We will show that our estimators
\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}
and
\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}
consistently estimate
{\mathbf{R}}{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{R}}}^{*}
and
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{*}
, respectively. To this end, the following assumptions for the unit-root setting are required.

Assumption 7
.

Let the stationary process
{\mathbf{w}}_{t}=\operatorname{vec}(\mathbf{F}_{t})-\operatorname{vec}(\mathbf{F}_{t-1})\in\mathbb{R}^{kr}
, for
t=2,3,...,T
. We assume that
{\mathbf{w}}_{t}=\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}{\mathbf{G}}_{s}{\mathbf{u}}_{t-s}
, where
{\mathbf{u}}_{t}
is a
kr
-dimensional i.i.d. random vector with zero mean, positive definite covariance matrix
\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{u}}
, and finite fourth moments. The coefficient matrices
\{{\mathbf{G}}_{s}\}
are
kr\times kr
-dimensional and satisfy

\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}s\|{\mathbf{G}}_{s}\|_{2}<\infty,\quad\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}\overset{\triangle}{=}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}{\mathbf{G}}_{s}\succ 0,

where
\succ 0
denotes positive definiteness.

Assumption 8
.

For the idiosyncratic component
{\mathbf{E}}_{t}
, we assume (a) The process
\operatorname{vec}(\mathbf{E}_{t})
is stationary, independent of
{\mathbf{F}}_{t}
, and
\alpha
-mixing with the mixing coefficient
\alpha(h)
satisfying
\sum_{h=1}^{\infty}\alpha(h)^{1-2/\gamma}<\infty
, where
\gamma>2
is given in Assumption
1
;

(b)
\mathbb{E}[e_{t,ij}]=0
and
\mathbb{E}|e_{t,ij}|^{8}\leq C
, for
i\in[p]
,
j\in[q]
, and
t\in[T]
;

(c)
\mathbb{E}\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\mathbf{C}^{j}e_{t,ij}\|_{2}^{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)
for
i\in[p]
and
t\in[T]
,
\mathbb{E}\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathbf{R}^{i}e_{t,ij}\|_{2}^{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)
for
j\in[q]
and
t\in[T]
, and
\mathbb{E}\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{pq}}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sum_{j=1}^{q}(\mathbf{R}^{i})^{\top}\mathbf{C}^{j}e_{t,ij}\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)
for
t\in[T]
.

Assumption 9
.

We assume the eigenvalues of
\bm{\Omega}_{{\mathbf{R}}}^{1/2}\widetilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{FC}_{i}}^{*}\bm{\Omega}_{{\mathbf{R}}}^{1/2}
and
\bm{\Omega}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{1/2}\widetilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{FR}_{j}}^{*}\bm{\Omega}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{1/2}
are both positive, bounded, and distinct, for
i\in[s_{1}]
and
j\in[s_{2}]
. The matrices
\widetilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{FC}_{i}}^{*}=\int_{0}^{1}\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}(u)\bm{\Omega}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{i}}\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}(u)^{\top}\text{d}u
and
\widetilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{FR}_{j}}^{*}=\int_{0}^{1}\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}(u)^{\top}\bm{\Omega}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{j}}\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}(u)\text{d}u
, where
\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}(u)={\mathbf{W}}(u)+\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}{\mathbf{W}}(t/T)
and
{\mathbf{W}}(\cdot)
is an
k\times r
matrix of Brownian motions with the covariance of
\text{vec}({\mathbf{W}}(\cdot))
being
\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{u}}\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}^{\top}
.

Assumption
7
, adapted from
31
, controls the magnitude of
{\mathbf{w}}_{t}
and
{\mathbf{F}}_{t}
and ensures that
{\mathbf{F}}_{t}
satisfies the basic condition of unit-root processes discussed in
3
. Assumption
8
(a)-(b) replaces the conditions on
{\mathbf{E}}_{t}
in Assumptions
1
-
2
, while Assumption
8
(c) corresponds to Assumption
5
. Similar to Assumption
6
, Assumption
9
controls the magnitude of
{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}^{*}
and
{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}^{*}
, thereby also bounding
{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{R}}}^{*}
and
{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{*}
. The distinct eigenvalue condition also guarantees the asymptotic stability of
{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{i}}^{*}
and
{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}}^{*}
, as well as of
{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{R}}}^{*}
and
{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{*}
.

The following theorems establish the consistency of our estimators for unit-root processes, mirroring the results for stationary processes in Theorems
1
–
3
.

Theorem 6
.

Under Assumptions
3
-
4
and
7
-
9
with known
k
and
r
, as
\underline{m},\underline{n},T\rightarrow\infty
, we have

\frac{1}{p}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{R}}-\mathbf{RH}_{\mathbf{R}}^{*}\right\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{2}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(\frac{1}{\underline{m}T^{2}}+\frac{1}{p^{2}T^{2}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{q}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{C}}-\mathbf{CH}_{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\right\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{2}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(\frac{1}{\underline{n}T^{2}}+\frac{1}{q^{2}T^{2}}\right).

Consequently,

\frac{1}{p}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{R}}-\mathbf{RH}_{\mathbf{R}}^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(\frac{1}{\underline{m}T^{2}}+\frac{1}{p^{2}T^{2}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{q}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{C}}-\mathbf{CH}_{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(\frac{1}{\underline{n}T^{2}}+\frac{1}{q^{2}T^{2}}\right).

Theorem 7
.

Under Assumptions
3
-
4
and
7
-
9
with known
k
and
r
, as
\underline{m},\underline{n},T\rightarrow\infty
, we have

\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}-(\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{R}}^{*})^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{t}[(\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{C}}^{*})^{-1}]^{\top}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\underline{m}}T}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\underline{n}}T}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{pq}}\right).

Theorem 8
.

Under Assumptions
3
-
4
and
7
-
9
with known
k
and
r
, as
\underline{m},\underline{n},T\rightarrow\infty
, we have

\widehat{{\mathbf{S}}}_{t,ij}-{\mathbf{S}}_{t,ij}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\underline{m}}T}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\underline{n}}T}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{pq}}\right),\quad i=1,2,...,p,\quad j=1,2,...,q.

Remark 3
.

(i) Theorems
6
-
8
imply that our procedure achieves higher accuracy for unit-root nonstationary processes than for stationary processes. This improvement is driven by the stronger trending signals contained in
{\mathbf{F}}_{t}
, a phenomenon also noted in
31
;

(ii) The convergence rates of our loading matrix estimators are comparable to those in
31
, which conducts estimation on a single machine. Under the assumptions adopted in this paper, their estimators (mPCA) satisfy

\frac{1}{p}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{R}}-\mathbf{RH}_{\mathbf{R}}^{*}\right\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{2}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(\frac{1}{qT^{2}}+\frac{1}{pT^{2}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{q}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{C}}-\mathbf{CH}_{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\right\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{2}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(\frac{1}{pT^{2}}+\frac{1}{qT^{2}}\right).

This comparison indicates that our distributed procedure substantially improves computational efficiency while incurring only a slight loss in convergence rate.

5
Numerical Results

In this section, we assess the performance of our method in finite samples through simulation studies and real data applications.

5.1
Simulation studies

In the simulation studies, the observations
\{\mathbf{Y}_{t},t=1,2,...,T\}
are generated from model (
2.1
),
\mathbf{Y}_{t}=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{F}_{t}\mathbf{C}^{\top}+\mathbf{E}_{t}
, where the entries of
{\mathbf{R}}
and
{\mathbf{C}}
are independently drawn from
\mathcal{U}(-1,1)
with
({k},{r})=(3,3)
. The generation of
{\mathbf{F}}_{t}
and
{\mathbf{E}}_{t}
, along with the choices of
p
and
q
, is described later since they vary across experiments. We evaluate the estimation accuracy, asymptotic normality, and computational efficiency of our estimators, focusing on the following aspects:

1.

Accuracy of
\widehat{k}
and
\widehat{r}
. The row and column factor numbers,
k
and
r
, are estimated by
\widehat{k}
and
\widehat{r}
, as described in Section
2.4
. We report the frequencies of
(\widehat{k},\widehat{r})
.

2.

Accuracy of
\{\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{s_{1}}
and
\{\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{s_{2}}
. For any nonsingular matrix
{\mathbf{A}}
and
{\mathbf{B}}
, the column space distance is defined as

\mathcal{D}({\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{B}})=\left\|{\mathbf{B}}({\mathbf{B}}^{\top}{\mathbf{B}})^{-1}{\mathbf{B}}^{\top}-{\mathbf{A}}({\mathbf{A}}^{\top}{\mathbf{A}})^{-1}{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\right\|_{\mathsf{F}}.

(5.1)

The accuracy of
\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}_{i}
and
\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j}
is evaluated using
\mathcal{D}({\mathbf{R}},\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}_{i})
and
\mathcal{D}({\mathbf{C}},\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}_{j})
, for any
i\in[s_{1}]
and
j\in[s_{2}]
. We report the average and maximum distances between the local estimators and the true loading matrices.

3.

Accuracy of
\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}
,
\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}
,
\widehat{{\mathbf{F}}}_{t}
and
\widehat{{\mathbf{S}}}_{t}
. According to Theorems
1
-
3
,
\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}
,
\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}
, and
\widehat{{\mathbf{F}}}_{t}
estimate rotated
{{\mathbf{R}}}
,
{{\mathbf{C}}}
, and
{{\mathbf{F}}}_{t}
, respectively, while
\widehat{{\mathbf{S}}}_{t}
directly estimates
{{\mathbf{S}}}_{t}
. The accuracy of
\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}
,
\widehat{{\mathbf{C}}}
, and
\widehat{{\mathbf{F}}}_{t}
is measured using

\frac{1}{p}\|\widehat{\mathbf{R}}-\mathbf{RH_{R}}\|_{2}^{2}
,
\frac{1}{q}\|\widehat{\mathbf{C}}-\mathbf{CH_{C}}\|_{2}^{2}
, and
\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}-\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{R}}^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{t}(\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{C}}^{-1})^{\top}\|_{2}^{2}
, where
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathbf{R}}
and
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathbf{C}}
are computed as described in Section
3
. The accuracy of
\widehat{{\mathbf{S}}}_{t}
is assessed using
\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\widehat{\mathbf{S}}_{t}-\mathbf{S}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}
.

4.

Asymptotic normality of
\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}-{\mathbf{R}}{\mathbf{H}}_{{\mathbf{R}}}
. We examine the asymptotic normality of the first row,
\sqrt{\overline{m}T}(\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}^{1}-{\mathbf{R}}^{1}{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathbf{R}})
. Following Theorems
4
-
5
, its asymptotic covariance matrix is estimated by
\widehat{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{1}}
. Consequently, each element of
\widehat{\Sigma}_{{\mathbf{R}}_{1}}^{-1/2}\sqrt{\overline{m}T}(\widehat{{\mathbf{R}}}^{1}-{\mathbf{R}}^{1}{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathbf{R}})^{\top}
is expected to follow the standard normal distribution. For illustration, we present the density histogram of its first component.

5.

Computational efficiency. We compare the computing time required to estimate the loading matrices using our method and
\alpha
-PCA.

5.1.1
Accuracy of estimators

First, we assess the estimation accuracy of our method. Following the AR(1) processes used in
39
, we generate
{\mathbf{F}}_{t}
and
{\mathbf{E}}_{t}
with controlled temporal dependence:

\displaystyle\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{F}_{t})=0.1\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{F}_{t-1})+\bm{\eta}_{t},\quad\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{E}_{t})=\psi\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{E}_{t-1})+\bm{\eta}^{\prime}_{t},

(5.2)

where
\bm{\eta}_{t}
and
\bm{\eta}^{\prime}_{t}
are noise vectors with independent elements drawn from
\mathcal{N}(0,0.99)
and
\mathcal{N}(0,1-\psi^{2})
, respectively. The initial states are set to
\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{F}_{0})=\bm{0}
and
\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{E}_{0})=\bm{0}
. To evaluate the impact of temporal dependence on estimation accuracy, we consider
\psi=0.1
and
\psi=0.5
, representing weaker and stronger temporal dependence in
\mathbf{E}_{t}
.

We first consider the performance of estimating
(k,r)
, as inaccurate estimates of the number of factors can substantially impair subsequent estimation. Table
5.1
reports the frequencies of our estimators (on the central server) and
\alpha
-PCA estimators under several settings. As expected, accuracy improves as the data dimensions
(p,q)
and sample size
T
increase and the temporal dependence
\psi
decreases. Across all configurations of
p
,
q
,
T
, and
\psi
, our method achieves estimation accuracy comparable to that of
\alpha
-PCA.

), and entries of

We next study the accuracy of the local loading matrix estimators, which directly determines the accuracy of our global estimators. To evaluate the accuracy of these estimators, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{1}(\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{i})=\frac{1}{s_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{s_{1}}\mathcal{D}(\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{i},\mathbf{R}),\quad\mathcal{D}_{2}(\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{i})=\max_{1\leq i\leq s_{1}}\mathcal{D}(\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{i},\mathbf{R}),

(5.3)

which measure the average and maximum column space distances from the estimators to the true row loading matrix
{\mathbf{R}}
, where
\mathcal{D}(\cdot)
is defined in Equation (
5.1
). The quantities
\mathcal{D}_{1}(\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{j})
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}(\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{j})
are defined similarly. Table
5.2
reports these statistics, with visualizations provided in Figure
1
(
\psi=0.1
) and Figure B1 (
\psi=0.5
, in Appendix B). Overall, these results demonstrate the high accuracy of our local estimators across all settings.

We now assess the accuracy of our global estimators. Table
5.3
reports means and standard deviations, based on 200 repetitions, of
e_{{\mathbf{R}}}=\frac{1}{p}\|\widehat{\mathbf{R}}-\mathbf{RH_{R}}\|_{2}^{2}
,
e_{{\mathbf{C}}}=\frac{1}{q}\|\widehat{\mathbf{C}}-\mathbf{CH_{C}}\|_{2}^{2}
,
e_{{\mathbf{F}}}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}-\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{R}}^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{t}(\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{C}}^{-1})^{\top}\|_{2}^{2}
, and
e_{{\mathbf{S}}}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\widehat{\mathbf{S}}_{t}-\mathbf{S}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}
from our method and
\alpha
-PCA. Consistent with Theorems
1
-
3
, estimation accuracy improves as
p
,
q
, and
T
increase.
In addition, unlike the other estimators, the estimation accuracy of the common component decreases sharply with higher temporal dependence
\psi
in the idiosyncratic term
{\mathbf{E}}_{t}
, as theoretically expected. Across all settings, our method achieves accuracy comparable to that of
\alpha
-PCA in estimating the loading matrices
{\mathbf{R}}
and
{\mathbf{C}}
, factor matrix
{\mathbf{F}}_{t}
, and common component
{\mathbf{S}}_{t}
.

,
